### Board (Claude) — 2026-10-06 15:11 UTC

Logged as item 92 (pushed, commit 4a39e69). Here is where the speed of light stands on the board, starting with what passes.

**1. Light speed is a local limit, not a distance limit.**
- In relativity, nothing outruns light *where it is*.
- The board's own rule says the same: D13 bounds information at c *in the space its carrier moves through*.
- A warp doesn't push anything past light locally. It changes the geometry between the two places.
- That is why the board, on your item 82, reads the warp's v as the shape's strength rather than a speed. In Alcubierre's metric, v sets how strongly space is curved, not how fast anything runs.

**2. Arriving "before light" is a question of route, not of speed.**
- carrier.py made this exact. A path beats light along our plane only if it is optically shorter: shorter in the sense light itself uses to measure distance.
- Your corridor, if it beats light, is such a short route. Since item 90 it is a connection that contains both positions, not a vehicle, and nothing traverses it.
- On your path, the "speed" of a trip becomes two things: how short the corridor makes the separation, and how long the hold lasts.

**3. Where light speed still appears, it shows up as a threshold, not a wall.**
- In escape.py, below light strength a bulk field carries the shape with positive energy.
- Above light strength the field needs a stress that can take either sign (open, BULK5-O4).
- So c marks where the shape's demand changes character. That is the board's current answer to your item 81: "Why does light speed matter if speed is not part of the warp process?" At the level of total energy it doesn't appear at all.

**4. What is ruled out, as the boundary.**
- A bulk that is lopsided everywhere would have shown in GW170817, where gravity and light arrived together from 130 million light years away. That bound covers only a lopsided bulk, not a local corridor.
- In the board's flat-plane RS1, no shortcut exists at all.

**5. One thread the board hasn't touched.**
- In relativity, a connection that beats light can look, in some observers' frames, like an arrival before departure.
- Your charter carries "maybe messages can travel into the past". Your H-BRIEF-HOLD says the hold is "relative".
- Whether a corridor can be made more than once (item 90) without allowing such loops is unexamined.

Where would you like to take it?
- **(a)** What fixes light speed inside the corridor, and so how short it makes the separation.
- **(b)** Light strength as the threshold in the shape's demand.
- **(c)** Causality: what a corridor that beats light means for "before" and "after", given your "relative".
- **(d)** Something else you have in mind.

The verifier on the shortest-hold result is still running. I'll bring its findings when it reports.

**What follows:**
- **If the teleport's "now" is one shared now for the whole dimension, your teleportation is causally safe, however many times a corridor is made.**
  - A return keyed to that shared now arrives at the moment of sending, never before.
  - Corridors all keyed to one frame never form a loop: 0 of 200 networks tested, in exact arithmetic.
  - This is also the published resolution: Liberati, Sonego and Visser show that "there can be no paradox if, in one particular reference frame", signals only go forward in time.
- **The board already has a candidate for that shared frame:** the cosmic rest frame, which the Sun moves through at 370 km/s. Your charter's own hypothesis, "a preferred frame exists", is what this needs.
- **What "all observers" costs.** For two separate places, "instantaneous" holds exactly in only one frame. Observers moving with the Sun would see a teleport to Proxima as about 1.9 days apart (1.65×10⁵ s): they disagree on timing, but never see a loop.

**What is ruled out:** if each teleport were instantaneous in its own object's frame, two objects moving relative to each other could send something back before it left. That happens at any relative speed at all; across Proxima it would arrive 1.9 days early.

Your words name both "relative to the object teleporting" and "all observers of the same dimension". Under relativity those agree only if one frame serves the whole dimension. **What your horizon picture gives:**
- **Your corridor is in this family of solutions.** Bronnikov and Kim's throat has members with a horizon outside the throat and no singularity, for masses r₀/2 < m < 2r₀/3. The paper calls these *"a nonsingular black hole with a wormhole throat … inside the horizon"*.
  - The solution is symmetric, so there is a horizon on each side, *"Horizons have 2 sides"*.
  - An observer at position 1 sees only their own mouth.
- **The pull our plane reads is exactly the horizon's own mass.** If the README sits on the horizon (the surface position 1 sees), the corridor's least cost is exactly the √N floor: **√(N h c⁵ ln2/(8π²G)) = 4.59404002×10⁸ J × √N**.
  - So the floor was always a horizon.
  - Bekenstein's bound, which is the most information a region of given size and energy can hold, is then met exactly.
  - If the README sits on the throat instead, the cost is pinned between the floor and 4/3 of it. For the core README that is 2.406×10¹⁶ to 3.208×10¹⁶ J, which straddles the 0.1c trip's 3.169×10¹⁶ J.
- **The family's own "no singularity" condition is the Penrose inequality.** That theorem says a horizon can't hold more mass than the total. Here the total exceeds the horizon's mass exactly when the solution has no singularity.
- **Item 102 splits across the two members.**
  - In your horizon corridor, the **reciprocal** is exact: energy × size = N × 3.48772679×10⁻²⁷ J·m. The README's size fixes the product, so the mass is the inverse of the size.
  - The **negative**, which you lean to, is exact only in the member with no horizon. There every reading our plane takes is negative: the total is zero, the pull is −2r₀c⁴/G, and light bends as if the mass were negative.
- **The twelve trajectories** are the 12-vector the board already carries: [M, R, K, T, CP, α_s, Z0, Λ, G, G_F, G_θ, v].

## Questions only you can answer
1. **C8P-O9:** Is your one exact energy (item 133) the E(N) at the bound? The board assumed it is, and E rests on that.
2. **C8P-O8:** The passage readings use a light ray's own energy scale, which is a free coefficient under item 125. Should we name a specific ray, or rule that the readings don't need one?
3. **C8P-O3:** What is the extra dimension's scale k for our universe today? The layers off our plane need a value for it, measured or ruled.
4. **C8P-O4:** You ruled there's no loop (item 128). So what sits at the two ends of the extra dimension?
5. **COPY-O8:** What does the device at position 2 weigh? Nothing anywhere specifies it, and it sets k*.
6. **S1B-O1 / BULK3-O1:** Is the README classical or quantum? Item 131 says "binary code", which reads as classical. A classical pattern gets copied unless the original is erased; an unknown quantum pattern gets moved.

## Real obstacles: a wall, not just unfinished work
- **The throat sits at the excluded member.** r₀ = 2m is exactly the case Bronnikov–Kim rule out. Nobody has shown it is stable or free of fine-tuning (C8P-O5).
- **The two planes can't yet be joined at one place.** You ruled that they coincide (item 127). The board's opposite-tension sheets fail the bulk's equations unless k = 0 (C8P-O2).
- **The global bulk can't simply be evolved.** Evolving it off a plane is not a well-posed problem (Maartens p.11). What exists is a series near the plane, nothing global (C8P-O1).
- **Wall 9:** Nothing yet takes us from a bridge that can't be crossed to your corridor (C8O-O4).
- **Reading the object:** the only demonstrated read at synapse resolution destroys the tissue (COPY-O3).

## Loose ends the board can compute without you
- C8O-O1: how the inflow and outflow join the throat, and where the null-energy violation enters.
- C8O-O2: a time-dependent model of position 1's horizon ending and position 2's arising.
- C8P-O7: the paths of light rays that leave the plane.
- C8O-O7: the bits a destination's trajectories carry.
- COPY-O2 and COPY-O5: the recipe's size, and how much a site's composition adds to the README.
- C8O-O9: whether the one-way passage is geodesically complete. X7 already shows an infinite one-way chain; completeness itself is still open.

## Need a source or a measurement
- C8P-O6: a 5D censorship theorem.
- C8O-O3: how many ebits already join two places.
- C8O-O8: whether there is a maximum power.
- COPY-O1: what identity needs beyond the cortical wiring.
- COPY-O4: what Proxima b or d is made of.
- COPY-O7: detecting single gravitons.
- G's uncertainty, which only a better measurement narrows.

- **Your coinciding planes join consistently.** Two flat planes at one place are consistent if their tensions add to the Randall–Sundrum value. They then share one geometry, which fits *"different views of the same object"*.

### Board (Claude) — 2026-10-07 03:09 UTC

D and I are done and pushed (4a2316d), each checked by an independent verifier. Before I start C, I need two answers from you, because they decide how C is built. One of the findings is a serious problem for the corridor as currently modelled.

## Your particle question
- **Electron:** all of its mass comes from the Higgs field, as the Standard Model has it. That link is not yet confirmed by experiment.
- **Neutron:** about 9%, the same as the proton. The 9% comes from a lattice calculation of the neutron and proton together (Yang et al., arXiv:1808.08677), and it counts the quark masses the Higgs field sets. The rest is the strong force's field energy, which depends on the Higgs field only indirectly.
- **W and Z bosons, top, bottom, tau:** all of their mass, confirmed at colliders.
- **Photon and gluon:** none; they are massless.
- **Neutrinos:** unknown.
- **For a 70 kg body**, the Higgs-set terms come to about 6.3 kg, roughly 9%.

On antimatter: your *"neither created nor destroyed, only reorganized"* settles it. The copy's particles are the stock reorganized, so no particle count changes. The released energy goes in as energy (motion and field energy), not as new matter.

## D: censorship
- **The theorem on the plane itself does not apply.** It needs the null energy condition along the passage. The plane reads that condition as broken, −0.826 per leg, and the break comes entirely from the bulk's geometry, not from any matter.
- **The finite-boundary theorems (Chruściel–Galloway–Solis) do not apply, because your extra dimension is unbounded** (answer 9). In a compact extra dimension, one of them would forbid any passage from one end of the corridor to the other.
- **The bulk theorems (Gao–Wald, Galloway et al.) are not settled.** They depend on two things:
  - the sign of your plane's tension;
  - where the bulk ends, which building it (C) will decide.
- **Your "an NEC is never violated" holds in five dimensions only if your plane has positive tension.** My first draft claimed it held outright, and I've withdrawn that.

## I: k and an observable
- **The dark-energy length (85 μm) is ruled out as k's scale.** It predicts gravity 1% stronger at 1.2 mm, and torsion-balance data fit Newton's law there.
- **The remaining range is from the Planck length up to a few μm.** If k sits near the top of that range, short-range gravity tests would see the force grow as 1/r⁴. That shape would be the second plane's signature.
- **The corridor bends light 9/8 as much as a plain mass with the same pull** (γ = β = 5/4). That agrees with the 2002 paper that introduced this metric (Casadio–Fabbri–Mazzacurati).

## The problem
In the standard one-plane bulk (Randall–Sundrum II), a mass on the plane changes the far field only very weakly (Maartens–Koyama, at second post-Newtonian order). That keeps γ at 1. Numerical brane black holes behave the same way: large ones look like ordinary black holes, small ones look five-dimensional (Figueras–Wiseman).

So **a static corridor with γ = 5/4 is not what that bulk produces, at any size** except possibly one close to the bulk's own scale. I see three ways out:
- (a) **the corridor exists only during the brief hold,** so results about static, long-lived objects don't bind it;
- (b) **the bulk holds more than one plane** (your infinite planes);
- (c) **the throat sits near the bulk's scale.** But the throat's size is set by N, so that could only hold for one README size.

## Questions
1. **Is the corridor a lasting static geometry, or does it exist only for the hold?** If only for the hold, way out (a) applies, and C means building a bulk that changes over time.
2. **Is your plane the positive-tension plane or the negative-tension one?** Positive keeps the null energy condition everywhere in five dimensions. Negative is how the board read Randall–Sundrum's two-plane model earlier, and there the condition fails on our plane.
3. **The Higgs field is already non-zero everywhere** (Salam et al., arXiv:2207.00478). What does "activates the Higgs field" mean on top of that: a disturbance of the field, or something else?
4. **Which scale for k should we hypothesize:** the top of the allowed range (a few μm, testable on a bench soon) or the Planck length (invisible to any experiment)?

**What it proves.** Theorem D covers your corridor (eq. 17 at r₀ = 2m) on a Randall–Sundrum plane, with a vacuum bulk that wall C showed exists near the plane. The selftest passes 7/7.

1. **The passage is a light ray of the bulk too.** This holds because the plane's extrinsic curvature K is proportional to its metric.
2. **In five dimensions the null energy along the passage is exactly zero.** The plane's apparent violation is, point by point, the bulk pulling neighbouring rays toward the plane: R⁵(n,k,n,k) = −R⁴(k,k) = 2r″/r, which is positive everywhere. This is the exact form of the board's reading of your items 117 and 120: the condition holds in five dimensions and only appears violated on the plane.
3. **Integrated, that pull equals the plane's whole deficit.** The total is Q = 8/3 − (4/(3√3))·artanh(√3/2) = 1.652872/m. That is exactly twice `coin.py`'s −0.826436 per leg.
4. **That pull is enough to refocus the rays.** A short lemma, proved in the note, shows that a positive total always brings neighbouring rays back together.
5. **Where they refocus.** Any point of the passage more than r_* = 2.21007m out on our side has a refocusing point on the far side. As the starting point recedes to infinity, that point tends to r_* on the far side. Two independent calculations give r_*, mirrored across the throat. Points closer than r_* on our side, and all points on the far side, have none.
6. **So the passage is not the fastest route in five dimensions.** Past a refocusing point, a timelike curve through the bulk joins the two ends (a standard result, Hawking–Ellis 4.5.12; keeping it on one side of the plane is my adaptation).

**What it means for censorship.** Two premises of a five-dimensional censorship theorem now hold: null energy for the combined, positive-tension plane, and the generic condition along the passage, which `censor.py` had only called "plausible". So any such theorem could stop the corridor only through two remaining premises:
- the five-dimensional spacetime being globally hyperbolic;
- the plane's two ends being distinct ends of the bulk.

Both are wall C's global questions, so wall D now waits on wall C. What I have not done is prove a full five-dimensional censorship theorem for a bulk bounded by a plane; nothing READ supplies one either. Your item 127 (the two positions coincide) is where the second premise could fail, but that isn't decided here.

I've written the theorem wall D was missing and pushed it (`bulk/censor5d.py`, selftest 4/4, and `bulk/CENSOR5D.md`, commit 4109816). An independent check is still running; it covers this theorem, Theorem D and the two wall-C pieces.

**The result.** In a five-dimensional spacetime that is globally hyperbolic, with your positive-tension plane, the corridor's two ends cannot be two separate ends of the bulk. Either they are one end of the bulk, joined through the deep bulk where the bulk ends, or one of two other premises fails (below).

**Theorem W.** Chruściel–Galloway–Solis's Theorem 3.1 (arXiv:0808.3233v2 pp.3–5, READ) says: a region with null energy, a Cauchy time function, and far boundaries that are untrapped (±θ± > 0, uniformly in time) and have compact time slices allows no causal curve from one boundary to another. Its premises stand as follows for your corridor:
- **Null energy (W1): met.** Your positive tension (item 139, DOORS K1) gives it. Their theorem assumes a smooth metric, so I read the thin plane as the limit of a thick one (H-THICK-PLANE, the board's).
- **Global hyperbolicity (W2): assumed, not shown.**
- **Far boundaries that exist and are untrapped (W3): computed for Randall–Sundrum II.** A 3-sphere centred on the plane has ±θ± = 3/R exactly, at every point, which is as untrapped as a sphere in flat space. As a control, a sphere centred on the AdS boundary gives θ = 0, which fails the strictness their Remark 3.2 says is necessary.
- **The passage crosses one sphere inward and the other outward.** So it is exactly the kind of curve the theorem forbids. Theorem D adds timelike routes through the bulk between the two ends.

**What is left for the corridor.** Your rulings that null energy is never violated (items 117 and 120) keep the energy route closed. Exactly three ways remain:
- **(a) The plane's two ends are one end of the bulk,** joined through the deep bulk. The wall C far-field result (GLOBALBULK G3) reached the same requirement independently.
- **(b) A far end fails to be untrapped.** Randall–Sundrum ends are untrapped, so this would need a far end that behaves worse than flat space.
- **(c) The five-dimensional spacetime is not globally hyperbolic.**

A compact extra dimension would forbid the passage outright (their Theorem 5.2); your answer 9 (item 136, an unbounded dimension) is what keeps that away.

Wall D therefore now turns on whether a bulk exists in which your two ends are one end, which is wall C's global question. My reading that (a) is a geometric form of your item 127 ("the positions coincide") is the board's, and the note says so.

- **Geometry:** Bronnikov–Kim's eq. (17) at r₀ = 2m, with E = √(N h c⁵ ln2/(8π²G)) = 459,404,002.42 J × √N.

- **Bulk:** a five-dimensional bulk carries the corridor, with positive energy everywhere and k fixed by the work.
- **Information:** the passage is N bits of entanglement.
- **Energy:** one exact energy, released into position 2 at the closing.
- **Reconstruction:** exact rearrangement at position 2, after which position 2's laws govern.

### Board (Claude) — 2026-10-07 19:36 UTC

The short version: B4 is blocked by a censorship theorem. That leaves exactly one route your rulings allow, and we can't yet build the five-dimensional geometry that route needs.

**1. The theorem that blocks the corridor**

Chruściel–Galloway–Solis, Theorem 3.1 (arXiv:0808.3233, READ at source), applied to the corridor in `CENSOR5D.md`. Suppose a 5D spacetime meets all three of these conditions:
- null energy holds at every point;
- it is globally hyperbolic (no causal pathologies);
- it has two separate "ends", each closed off by a far boundary that isn't trapped.

Then **no causal curve can run from one end to the other.** The corridor's passage is exactly such a curve, if positions 1 and 2 sit in two different ends of the bulk.

Your rulings fix the first condition: null energy is never violated (items 117 and 120) and the tension is positive (item 139). So one of the other conditions has to fail. That gives four ways out:
- **(a)** the plane's two ends are **one end** of the bulk, joined through the deep bulk;
- **(b)** no far boundary is untrapped;
- **(c)** the bulk isn't globally hyperbolic;
- **(d)** the thin plane isn't the limit of a smooth plane that keeps null energy at every point.

(c) means causal pathology. (d) would undercut your "an NEC is never violated". (b) is where a lasting corridor would end up, and item 136 answer 3 rules a lasting corridor out. So **(a) is the route the board is on**. Reading it as your "the positions coincide" (item 127) is the board's own interpretation.

**2. Why (a) isn't free: three things it needs**

- **The topology.** With one end, the same paper's Theorem 3.5 applies. The passage must be deformable, with its endpoints fixed, into the far boundary, so the throat's handle has to be filled in through the bulk. On the plane the corridor is a handle; in 5D it must not be one. Topologically this looks fine: the (u, y) half-plane times a sphere, R²×S², is contractible.
- **The rulings it must sit with.** One end of the bulk has to be reconciled with:
  - separate universes (item 101 answer 5);
  - no loop (item 128);
  - infinitely many planes (items 123, 124, 136 answer 9, 138), each of which is another boundary the theorem has to account for.

  The natural reading is that separate universes are separate planes inside one connected bulk. That reading is the board's, not yet shown.
- **The geometry.** This is the real obstruction. Someone has to produce an actual 5D vacuum geometry that:
  - extends the local bulk (B2, proved only near the plane);
  - matches Bronnikov–Kim's eq. (17) on the plane;
  - is regular everywhere.

  We don't have one, and two computed results push against it:
  - **The far field doesn't fit (`GLOBALBULK.md` G2).** Eq. (17)'s bulk pull at large r falls off as 1/r. A Randall–Sundrum bulk can only produce ℓ²/r³. So a corridor that lasted could not sit in an ordinary Randall–Sundrum bulk.
  - **The near-throat test is undecided.** Today's run (the order-11 series) loses control about one throat-radius into the bulk. That is roughly where g_tt and g_rr fall to zero together. This is either the bulk's own horizon, which is harmless, or a curvature singularity. A singularity would also break global hyperbolicity, which drops us into escape (c).

**3. Where I think we've been asking the wrong question**

The curvature test was run on a **static** bulk: a corridor standing forever. Your rulings say it doesn't stand forever:
- it is single-use (item 90);
- it lasts 28.5 corridor clocks (O3);
- it widens a bridge that was already there (B7);
- it does "not indefinitely hold something" (item 136 answer 3).

So B4 may not need a static global bulk at all. It may need something else: start from a regular bulk with the bridge already there, evolve it forward through the widening and the 28.5-clock hold, and show three things:
- the evolution stays regular;
- it keeps one end;
- it closes again.

That is a different calculation: evolving initial data forward, rather than extending the plane's geometry outward. The static near-throat singularity, if it is real, then counts for us: it is the reason a lasting corridor can't exist, which you already ruled. The catch is that 28.5 clocks is longer than the 1.3–3.2 clocks the local bulk's own data control (O3d), so the evolution can't be settled locally alone.

**Where I'd like your view before we compute**

When you said the corridor's two positions coincide (item 127), did you picture them as one place reached through the extra dimension? That is escape (a), and it would let us state B4 as a theorem about one end and a filled handle. Or did you picture something else? Separately, does reframing B4 as a brief forward evolution, rather than a standing static bulk, match how you see the corridor? If both answers are yes, the next calculation is clear. I would set up regular starting data with the bridge already there and show that the evolution keeps one end and stays free of singularities through the hold.

### Board (Claude) — 2026-10-07 19:43 UTC

I've recorded your answers as item 152, verbatim. Here is what they change, and the plan I'd follow.

**What your first answer settles**

"Two separate positions connected by/reached through a dimension" is precisely escape (a). In the censorship theorem, "one end" doesn't mean one place. It means the far regions around positions 1 and 2 are connected *through the bulk*. Two separate positions on the plane, joined through the extra dimension, is one end in that sense.

So Theorem 3.1 no longer forbids the corridor. What applies instead is the same paper's Theorem 3.5: the passage must be deformable, with its endpoints fixed, into a route through the bulk. That is your "reached through a dimension" stated as mathematics. The topological half of B4 now rests on your word plus a check the board can do.

**What the brief evolution buys**

- **The topology is fixed before the opening.** A well-behaved (globally hyperbolic) evolution can't change the topology of space (Geroch's theorem). So the one-end connection must already exist before the corridor opens. That is B7's bridge that already exists, joined through the bulk. The widening changes its size, not its topology. B7 and item 152 then describe the same starting state.
- **The question becomes local.** Over 28.5 clocks, the opening can only affect bulk within its light-reach. Everything beyond stays the ordinary, regular bulk it was. So regularity only needs checking in a bounded region around the throat.
- **What it doesn't buy: the near-throat question.** Near the throat the bulk responds within a few clocks: the local span is 1.3–3.2 clocks. The hold lasts 28.5. So the depth where the static series broke down (about 1m) is well inside the reach.
  - The question changes from "is the standing bulk regular?" to "does the evolving bulk stay regular for 28.5 clocks?"
  - That is a weaker demand. A singularity that would take longer than the hold to form never forms.

**Something the static test exposed: the scale ℓ**

- **The ℓ = r₀ test I ran is probably the wrong regime.** At ℓ = r₀ the series looked bad about a throat-radius in. In the opposite limit, ℓ much larger than r₀, it looked clean to at least 1.6m.
- **What the record says about ℓ:**
  - Measurement bounds ℓ only from above, at about 50 µm (Lee et al., READ in `KSCALE.md`).
  - At the example README, r₀ is about 4×10⁻²⁸ m.
  - So unless ℓ sits within about seven orders of magnitude of the Planck length, ℓ is far larger than r₀.
  - The board's earlier hypothesis that ℓ is much smaller than r₀ (H-ONE-BIT-SCALE) was withdrawn.
- **A consequence for B6.** If B4's regularity turns out to depend on ℓ, then B6's statement that "every clause is free of k's scale" needs a qualifier. Your item 136 answer 8 leaves ℓ to measurement, so the board should treat B4 in the regime measurement allows and say which regime that is.

**The plan, in order:**
1. **Topology lemma.** Given item 152, the plane's two ends form one end of the bulk, and the passage can be deformed through the bulk (Theorem 3.5). This is deduction plus a structural check.
2. **Topology is preserved.** The starting state is B7's bridge joined through the bulk, and the evolution keeps one end. This means reading Geroch at source.
3. **Light-reach.** Compute, in the bulk metric, the region the opening can affect over 28.5 clocks: how deep, and how far along the plane.
4. **Regularity inside that region.** This is the hard part.
   - First, settle the static near-throat question in the regime ℓ ≫ r₀, by a method that doesn't rely on the Taylor series. I'd march the bulk equations out from the plane numerically, with the ℓ = r₀ case as a control.
   - Then, compare how long the bulk takes to settle near the throat with the 28.5-clock hold.
   - Solving the full evolution is numerical-relativity scale and beyond what I can do here. I'll say plainly where the argument stops.

Unless you want a different order, I'll start with steps 1–3. They're short and should turn the topological half of B4 green or show why not. Then I'll take on step 4.

**What passes**
- **B4a, proved (from the construction itself), assuming B4b holds.** Your "two separate positions connected by/reached through a dimension" is exactly the way past the censorship theorem: the two positions sit in one connected region of the bulk. The passage can then be bent, endpoints fixed, into a route through the bulk. That is the theorem's own requirement, and your words drawn as a curve.
  - **Controls:** a bulk joined only at the throat (the other reading of "connected by a dimension") fails, and so does a bulk with holes cut out inside the far boundary. Neither allows the bend.
- **The topology is fixed before the corridor opens.** This is READ at source: Aké Hau–Flores–Sánchez, arXiv:1808.04412, prove that a well-behaved spacetime keeps the same shape of space at every time. So the connection through the bulk already exists before the opening: it is B7's pre-existing bridge, and the opening only widens it.

**What rests on the board's model**
- **B4c, the far boundary.** I computed it for B4a's own boundary surface, in a model of my own rather than an exact solution. The model's control reproduces the earlier 3/R result.
  - **The new condition:** the boundary has to sit beyond the hold's reach and inside half the bulk's curvature length ℓ. That needs ℓ greater than about 57 corridor units.
  - Measurement bounds ℓ only from above, at tens of micrometres, while the corridor at the example README is about 10⁻²⁸ m. So the condition is met by an enormous margin unless ℓ is extremely small, and it fails at ℓ = r₀.
  - Two pieces are also my own assumptions:
    - After the corridor closes, its outgoing gravitational waves don't trap the boundary.
    - Eq. (17) applies only inside the hold's reach.

**What is open**
- **B4b: the bulk stays regular through the hold.** The plane is static during the hold, so near the throat the bulk must be the standing bulk, and it must be regular as deep as the hold's light can reach.
- **The depth needed:** my first figure of 14.24m was wrong. Near the throat, clocks run very slowly, so the depth is small: about 2m at r = 2.05m, rising to about 8m at r = 3m, for ℓ much larger than r₀.

|---|---|---|
| r = 2.05m (near the throat) | about 1.9–2.2m | 1.5m |
| r = 3m | about 8.2m | 2.25m |

- **What the series shows:** nothing it reaches is singular, but it doesn't reach far enough to settle B4b at any distance from the throat.
- **At ℓ = r₀** the series gives out where it would need to distinguish a curvature singularity from a harmless bulk horizon, so it can't say which.
- **To close B4b** I need a method that goes beyond the series: either a direct numerical solve of the standing bulk, or a resummed series with a bounded error.

**Two corrections the verifier made me take**
- I had called the ℓ = r₀ evidence "leaning singular". It isn't: the breakdown sits exactly at the series' own limit, where a singularity and a horizon look the same.
- B4 depends on ℓ, both the depth needed and the far-boundary condition. So B6's statement that "every clause is free of k's scale" no longer covers B4, and the theorem now says so.

### Board (Claude) — 2026-10-07 23:27 UTC

## O3's direct question

**Does the corridor stay open, unharmed, for as long as it has to?** It splits into two parts:

- **How long does it have to stay open?** That is the hold.
- **How long can it stay open?** That is the most time the corridor, and the bulk around it, can stay regular.

O3 is answered "yes" when the first number is no bigger than the second.

## How the question has been reshaped

**1. First form: guess the hold, then test survival.** The board took the hold as one orthogonal flip per bit, with each bit on its own share of the energy. That gave 28.48 clocks for every N. Survival was then checked in linear theory, and passed.

**2. The bulk answered the second part, and the guess failed.** B4 step 5 computed how long the static bulk stays regular: about 11.3 clocks. The 28.48-clock hold reaches the singular surface above the throat, so the per-bit guess was withdrawn. O3 became a window: the corridor survives any hold below 11.3 clocks, provided the hold falls inside it. Whether it does was left as an open requirement.

**3. Your item 158 gave the first part its own definition.** You said the hold lasts *"exactly as long as the write needs."* That turned "how long must it stay open?" into a physics question with an answer: how long does writing the README take, at the least?

**4. The question was simplified to two numbers.** One is the write's least time; the other is the bulk's limit of 11.3 clocks. Nothing else in O3 depends on a reading.

**5. The write's least time is now computed (not yet verified).**
- **The rate bounds didn't decide it.** These are limits on how fast information can move, and each gives a fixed number of clocks whatever N is.
- **What decided it is room.** Under your 115 (c) the README is the inflow, so it has to start no further away than light can travel in the write's time.
- **Inside that space** it can hold no more states than a free gas of the same energy. Holding N bits then needs about (N/Z)^(1/3) clocks, where Z counts the light particle types.

## The answer it now gives

- **On the static bulk: no,** for any README above about 7.4·Z bits. At the example README the write needs about 760,000 clocks against the bulk's 11.3. In seconds that is still only about 5×10⁻³¹ s.
- **Under your words, the static bulk is what gives way.** The bulk cannot sit still through the write. That answers your question (3) of item 158.

## What O3 has become

O3 is now one narrower question: **can the corridor, with its bulk evolving rather than static, stay regular for about (N/Z)^(1/3) clocks?**

The first part is computed and is waiting on the verifier, which is still running. The second part is B4d. So O3, Formation and Address now all rest on the same thing: the corridor's five-dimensional evolution through the write.

### Board (Claude) — 2026-10-08 02:08 UTC

I've recorded this as item 164 and worked it in `lemmas/o3_ground.py` (selftest 7/7, not yet verified). I re-read the Margolus–Levitin paper in full for it.

**Where you're right.** A ground state does oscillate in two ways:
- **Its phase turns.** Evolved, the ground state is just e^(−iE₀t/ħ) times itself.
- **Its position keeps a spread that never goes away.** For hydrogen's ground state it is (√3/2) Bohr radii.

**Where it doesn't follow.** Its energy does not oscillate:
- **No spread.** The ground state's energy spread is exactly zero, and its average energy never changes.
- **The phase's rate is not even fixed.** Shift the zero of energy, as Margolus–Levitin themselves do, and the phase turns at a different rate. Nothing observable changes.
- **No isolated state's energy oscillates.** Even in Margolus–Levitin's own fastest state, the average energy is exactly E at every moment. What oscillates there is the overlap with the starting state, not the energy.

So their p.9 line stands: an atom in an exact energy state never moves to a different state.

**The conflict I reported is gone anyway.** It belonged to the instant reading, which your item 163 set aside:
- **Why the instant clashed.** Changing state within h/(4E) needs an energy spread as large as the energy itself. That is what clashed with your "one exact energy" (133) and the fixed size (162).
- **Why the whole write doesn't.** It lasts at least 200,000 clocks, so it needs a spread of only **5.2×10⁻²⁰ of E**.
- **So your exact energy holds to that,** and the throat's size holds to 1.3×10⁻¹² of a Planck length. Over that long a write the physics also applies, where the instant (6.9×10⁻⁵¹ s) was shorter than a Planck time.

**My reading of your "oscillating state" for the corridor:** it is in a near-exact energy state, exact to 5.2×10⁻²⁰, whose phase turns. I've named that as the board's reading, not yours.

**This changes no status.** O3 still depends on B4d, the question of whether the corridor's bulk stays regular, not on the write's energy.

**B4d stage 5 (`lemmas/b4d_stage5.py`, selftest 9/9, not yet verified).** It answers that question under your item 163:
- **The corridor is a through-flow.** A fixed size with the README flowing in means an equal amount must flow out, so the README passes from position 1 to position 2.
- **The bulk still meets a singular surface.** On the board's readings, that happens at every ℓ tested (∞, 2m, m, m/2), and the surface gets nearer the plane as ℓ falls.
- **It is reached fast.** Within about 13–22 clocks the curvature reaches 10⁵ or more, against a write of at least 200,000 clocks.
- **A second plane doesn't rescue it.** Closing the bulk with one would need real matter that breaks the null energy condition, which your items 117/120 rule out.

So your inclination in item 161 does not hold on those readings. What stage 5 refutes is the combination of readings it used, not the inclination itself, and five ways around it remain open:
- a plane with bulk on both sides;
- a curved closing wall;
- a through-flowing throat that isn't the static eq. (17) shape;

# Where the chain stands

## The theorem
A corridor of energy E carries an N-bit README from position 1 to position 2. On each plane it shows as eq. (17), an extremal throat of radius 2m, and through the five-dimensional bulk it is a passage. Every lemma has to be computed, READ at source, or deduced.

## The tally
| Status | Count | Lemmas |
|---|---|---|
| Green (proved or derived) | 28 | — |
| Readings | 2 | B4b, B4c |
| Nature's to decide | 1 | B6′, the bulk's scale k |
| Open | 3 | O3, B3, B4d |

All three open lemmas come down to one, B4d:
- O3 says the corridor survives its hold;
- B3 says a complete bulk exists;
- both close when B4d does.

## What your rulings have fixed
- **The hold is the write (158, 160).** It lasts at least 2.0×10⁵ clocks, the time the README needs to arrive.
- **One object, fixed size, held as one whole (162, 163).** "At once" means all together, not an instant.
- **The write's energy is settled (164).** The held README sits at exactly one energy E. The write needs an energy spread of only 5.2×10⁻²⁰ of E, and nothing swings back once the write ends.
- **The planes (127, 139, 152, 166).** Ours has positive tension and position 2's negative. They are joined through the extra dimension, and the corridor spans both at once.

## The question
**B4d: can the corridor's five-dimensional bulk stay regular for the whole write, at least 2×10⁵ clocks?** Your inclination is yes (161).

## What the board has found (stages 5–7, each verified once)
1. **The side of a plane decides it.** With eq. (17) on a plane, the bulk turns singular within 8–16 clocks on any side where the warp falls away from the plane. On a side where the warp grows it stays regular, on the evidence. This held at every ℓ tested, from 2m down to m/8.
2. **Every positive-tension plane has a side where the warp falls.** So our plane cannot carry eq. (17) regularly by itself.
3. **Position 2's plane alone could,** at its own ℓ (138), because its warp grows on both sides.
4. **Across both planes (166), every small-separation arrangement fails.** At least one bulk beyond a plane has its warp falling, and that bulk is singular:
   - directly, if it is ours;
   - by continuity if it is position 2's, which is a reading, not a proof.
5. **Two more routes are closed.** Closing the bulk with another plane would need matter that breaks the NEC, which your 117/120 rule out. And a flat position-2 plane can't hold its tension, so it would have to curve.

## Recorded, not repaired
Your 139's −1/3 counts position 2's plane twice. A single sheet carries −1/6. No conclusion depends on which count is right.

## What is still open (each could still make 161 true)
1. **The bulk built from data on the plane that differ by direction.** This is the general case, and the one remaining computation that could rescue a regular corridor.
2. **Separations close to the throat's own singular surface,** where its curvature is already thousands of times the black string's.
3. **Curved planes.**
4. **Bulks beyond the planes that aren't anti-de Sitter** (138).
5. **A bulk that isn't empty (158 (4)).** This runs against clause (B) as written.

## Next
A solver for the bulk grown from direction-dependent data on the plane, which tests route 1. If that bulk is regular, B4d, and with it O3 and B3, can go green. If it is singular too, the corridor's bulk cannot stay regular through the write on the board's readings, and what remains is to say which ruling or reading has to give.

### Board (Claude) — 2026-10-09 07:00 UTC

I recorded your thought word for word as item 169, marked as a thought for discussion and not a ruling, and started no new work on it. It turns out to be useful in three ways.

**1. Inside one universe, it follows from your own rulings.** Hawking's chronology protection theorem (1992) says a time machine of the usual kind can't form anywhere the null energy condition holds. Your items 117 and 120 say that condition is never actually broken. So "no time travel within one universe" is already a consequence of what you've ruled. It also agrees with what the board's math keeps finding: within one spacetime, there's no shortcut. The board hasn't yet read Hawking's paper first-hand, so for now that link is standard physics we haven't checked against the paper.

**2. It closes a loophole the board would otherwise have had to face.** In ordinary general relativity, any wormhole with both mouths in one universe becomes a time machine if you move one mouth: the twin-paradox clock lag turns into a time offset between the mouths (Morris, Thorne and Yurtsever, 1988). Your thought says that can never happen inside one universe; the attempt lands in a separate spacetime instead. So the corridor can never be a time machine in our universe, and grandfather-type paradoxes can't arise. Your version is also distinct from the two published answers:
- Novikov keeps one self-consistent timeline;
- Deutsch (1991) sends the traveller to another branch of the same quantum history;
- yours is a third option, a genuinely separate spacetime, which fits your item 138 (each universe its own laws).

**3. It sharpens the hierarchy that Phase 2 is built on:**
- **Same universe, same moment, different place:** only the address is involved, and by your item 116(a) an address isn't a trajectory. This is Phase 2.
- **A different moment or event:** a history trajectory, so a separate spacetime. This is Phase 3.
- **Different laws:** a law trajectory, which comes later.

So Phase 2's corridor joins positions 1 and 2 at the same moment, which matches your 126: "realized in the same place the position 1 occupies while the corridor exists". It also takes arrival time out of what Phase 2 measures, consistent with your 139(4).

**The one thing it leaves open.** "At the time of travel" needs a definite "now" at position 2, and in relativity a distant "now" depends on whose clock you use. For Proxima Centauri (4.25 light-years away, approaching at about 22.5 km/s, per Wolfram|Alpha):
- the Sun's "now" and Proxima's "now" differ by about 2.8 hours;
- Earth's orbit swings our own "now" through a range of roughly 0.1 to 5.5 hours over a year. That range is a rough estimate.

Combine this with your 119 (the address is exactly what the user inputs), and an input whose time is off by even that much would, by this thought, land in a separate spacetime. So it matters which clock defines "now" at the destination.

Since the corridor is one object (162), the natural candidate is that the corridor itself sets one "now" for both mouths. That's also how the Einstein–Rosen bridge in your PDF behaves: at a given instant it joins its two sides at a single shared time.

If you want to say which clock you meant, that would settle it. If not, nothing here needs an answer to keep going.

- **The object's clock is position 1's.** Its memory runs on its own elapsed time from departure, like the astronaut's. In the corridor that elapsed time is just the hold: at least 2.0×10⁵ of the board's time units. At the example README that is about 10⁻³¹ s, so the object arrives remembering essentially no time passing. Meanwhile position 2's clocks may read hours apart from position 1's at departure, depending on how the two were synchronised. That gap is the astronaut's mismatch.

That's a reading of your words, not something you said. The corridor runs one way, 1 → 2 (a seated result, plane.py P1), so going home takes a second corridor. On that leg position 1 is the destination, so position 1's clock governs.

If the two positions are moving toward each other, the round trip arrives home before you left. The amount is the |v|·D/c² from before: Proxima is approaching us at about 22.5 km/s, so that's about 2.8 hours early, with Earth's orbit moving it anywhere from minutes to about 5.5 hours. This is standard special relativity; it's the same mechanism that turns a moving wormhole mouth into a time machine.

**Why it's consistent.** Arriving 2.8 hours before you left is a time-displaced target. Your item 169 sends exactly that to a separate spacetime, not back into ours. So:
- a return that would arrive after you left (positions moving apart) comes home;
- a return that would arrive before you left lands elsewhere.

Item 170 alone would allow a loop in time; item 169 is what closes it, and within one universe the theory stays free of paradox.

**What it gives Phase 2.** The rule for "now" is settled: the destination's frame sets it, and the traveller keeps its own time. Arrival time is therefore not something Phase 2 has to model or measure. Nothing else was started; the two background workflows are still running.

I recorded the question as item 171. Short answer: yes, and on your own rulings it isn't something to derive after the warp work. Space-only teleportation is the first rung of the warp work, which is the Phase 2 being designed now.

**Why.** Put your rulings together and the corridor within one universe already is space-only teleportation:
- **What crosses is information.** Item 86, answers 6–7: the README, telling position 2 what to build "with what it has".
- **It arrives at position 2's present.** Item 170: position 2's clock.
- **The object keeps its own time.** Item 170: position 1's, like the astronaut.
- **Time is never traversed.** Item 169.

"Two positions, same moment, one universe" is exactly item 168's Phase 2. If Phase 2 comes out positive, that's a space-only teleporter; anything between universes or across time comes in later phases.

**How it compares with known physics** (standard results, not yet checked against the original papers by the board):
- **Quantum teleportation already has this shape** (Bennett et al., 1993). Shared entanglement plus a message rebuilds a state at the far end, and the original state is destroyed in the process. Your item 86.4 has the same ingredients: entanglement plus a trajectory.
- **The limit in physics is the message.** It travels at light speed or slower, so the transfer is never faster than light. In your theory the corridor replaces that channel, and whether such a channel can exist within one universe is precisely the open problem, B4d.
- **This bears on your item 86.8, "a copy/clone".** Physics forbids copying an unknown quantum state (the no-cloning theorem), and identical particles carry no identity beyond their state. So in physics "same or copy" comes down to whether the original state survives. If the full state moves, it cannot survive at both ends.

**What it hinges on:**
- **Phase 2's answer.** One universe is where the censorship theorems bite hardest: with the null energy condition in force, a route through one universe can't be a real shortcut unless one of the escapes the board has listed holds. That's open, and it's what Phase 2 tests.
- **Size is cheap in hold time.** The write's floor grows only as the cube root of the README's size (o3_write.py's bound, in the board's time units). A description a million times larger needs about 100 times the hold.
- **Round trips.** By the item 170 reasoning, an instant out-and-back between places moving toward each other would arrive home before you left, about 2.8 h early for Proxima. Item 169 sends such a return to a separate spacetime. A teleporter's return trip lands back in our universe only when the two places aren't closing on each other. This is the board's deduction from the destination-frame reading.

### Board (Claude) — 2026-10-09 14:51 UTC

No. Entanglement doesn't need negative tension, or negative energy of any kind. I recorded your question as item 175.

**What's in an atom.** An atom holds protons, neutrons and electrons, but no positron. A neutron and a positron do appear together in beta-plus decay: a proton turns into a neutron and emits a positron (and a neutrino). Their states come out entangled because momentum and spin are conserved, and every energy involved is positive. The answer is the same if you meant proton and electron. This is standard physics that the board hasn't checked against a paper.

**Where the board's negative tension comes from.** It's gravitational, not quantum. Away from our positive-tension plane the warp of the extra dimension can only keep falling; it can't turn back. So any plane facing ours across the bulk must read negative. That's Phase 2's result, and S15's coinciding sheet at −1.

**Where negative energy does meet entanglement.** It shows up when a connection between entangled things is made traversable:
- **Your theory:** the passage is N bits of entanglement (lemma I1, derived from your 137).
- **Entanglement alone:** two entangled black holes are joined by a bridge that can't be crossed (Maldacena–Susskind, which the board has read). Nothing passes; that's why entanglement can't send signals.
- **Making it crossable:** that needs negative null energy, supplied by a quantum coupling between the two ends (Gao–Jafferis–Wall, 2016; the board hasn't read that paper first-hand).

So entanglement itself is free. The cost comes when the connection has to carry the README, and that's exactly where your 117/120 say the null energy condition only appears to break.

**One computed fact with a familiar shape.** At coincidence, position 2's piece carries exactly the opposite surface stress of ours, so the pair nets to zero. Two entangled positions sitting on opposite sides is the shape of your 117 picture, which your 123 set aside. The board records it as a computed fact and does not read it as entanglement.

### Board (Claude) — 2026-10-09 14:53 UTC

If it is entanglement, there is standard physics for it, and it fits the corridor's geometry closely. I recorded the question as item 176, with the board's answer carried as a reading, H-PAIRING-IS-ENTANGLEMENT.

**Why it fits.**
- **The geometry matches.** The corridor's throat is AdS₂ × S² (Phase 2 S6, confirmed by Kaus–Reall). Global AdS₂ is exactly the geometry physicists use for two entangled systems joined by a wormhole. Maldacena and Qi (2018, read by the board): *"AdS2, viewed as a global spacetime, has two boundaries that are causally connected … It behaves like a traversable wormhole!"*
- **The ± pairing has a known counterpart.** In the standard entangled state of two such systems (the thermofield double), the two sides read opposite energies against their shared clock, netting to zero. Each side is positive by its own clock. That is the shape of the computed +σ/−σ pairing at coincidence. This is standard physics, not yet read first-hand.

**What would change if it is entanglement.**
- **Position 2's −1 becomes an appearance.** It would be the entangled partner's sign as seen from our side. Your 139(2) positivity would then have to hold in position 2's own frame. That frame needs position 2's own bulk on its far side, so the test lives between universes: phase 3 can compute it.
- **The cost moves.** Entanglement alone can't carry anything; it can't send signals. Carrying the README needs the two ends coupled, and that coupling produces negative null energy (Maldacena–Qi; Gao–Jafferis–Wall). In five dimensions that negative energy may not be quantum at all. Maldacena and Milekhin (2020, read by the board): *"what looks like a quantum Casimir energy in four dimensions is actually a classical effect in five dimensions."* In 4D it would appear broken; in 5D it would be ordinary bulk geometry. That is close to your 117/120.

**What wouldn't change.** Within one universe at our tension, exact coincidence still gives negative energy (S15).
